{\mathfrak{A}I=\mathfrak{A}b_{1}+\dots+\mathfrak{A}b_{s}}
. Since
I=R(\mathfrak{A}I)=R(\mathfrak{A}b_{1}+\dots+\mathfrak{A}b_{s})=\mathfrak{A}^{G}b_{1}+\dots+\mathfrak{A}^{G}b_{s}
,
I
is finitely generated over
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
is Noetherian.

(c)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be an algebra graded over
\mathbb{N}
(for simplicity of notations):
\mathfrak{B}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, with
{\mathfrak{B}_{m}.\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{m+n}}
. The augmentation ideal of
\mathfrak{B}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{+}:=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{+\infty}\mathfrak{B}_{n}
. If
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
is a Noetherian ideal of
\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathfrak{B}
is a finitely generated algebra over
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
. This is
[
99
, Lemma 2.4.5]
(in that statement
\mathfrak{B}
is commutative, but this hypothesis is not necessary for the proof).

(d)

Assume that
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
is graded over
\mathbb{N}
(for simplicity of notations):
\mathfrak{A}^{G}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathfrak{A}^{G}_{n}
with
{\mathfrak{A}^{G}_{0}=K}
. Then
\mathfrak{A}^{G}{}^{+}=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{+\infty}\mathfrak{A}^{G}_{n}
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
, which is Noetherian by (b) above. Applying
(c)
, we deduce that
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
is a finitely generated algebra over
K
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Consider the filtration
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
by the subspaces

{\mathcal{F}}^{[\mu]}=\bigoplus_{[\mu^{\prime}]\preceq[\mu]}C([\mu^{\prime}]),\qquad\mu\in P_{+}^{n},

where
P_{+}^{n}
is given the lexicographic partial order induced from
[\Lambda]
. It is easily seen that
\mathcal{F}
is an algebra filtration: the coregular actions
\rhd
,
\lhd
fix globally each component
C(\mu)
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, so the claim follows from (
2.9
), (
2.17
) and the fact that
C(\mu)\star C(\nu)\subset C(\mu+\nu)
for all
\mu,\nu\in P_{+}
. Denote by
{\rm Gr}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
the corresponding graded algebra. As a vector space, we have

{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})=\bigoplus_{[\mu]\in P_{+}^{n}}C([\mu]).

(3.14)

Because each space
C([\mu])
is stabilized by the coadjoint action of
U_{q}
, (
3.14
) has a key advantage on the refined decomposition (
3.11
). Indeed, since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is a
U_{q}
-module algebra, the action of
U_{q}
is well defined on
{\rm Gr}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
and gives it a structure of
U_{q}
-module algebra. As vector spaces, we have

{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}=\bigoplus_{[\mu]\in P_{+}^{n}}C([\mu])^{U_{q}}.

Now we can adapt the different steps (a)–(d) recalled above:

(a
′
)

The action of
U_{q}
on
{\rm Gr}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
is completely reducible. This follows from (
3.14
) and the fact that the spaces
C(\mu)
are finite-dimensional and thus completely reducible
U_{q}
-modules. We can therefore define the Reynolds operator as in (a),

R\colon\ {\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})\rightarrow{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}.

(b
′
)

{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
is Noetherian, because (
3.14
) shows it is filtered by
{\mathcal{F}}_{3}
, and the associated graded algebra
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{3}}({\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}))={\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{3}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
is Noetherian by Theorem
3.1
. As in (b), we deduce that
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}
is Noetherian. But
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}={\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)
, which implies that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
is Noetherian.

(c
′
)

(and (d
′
)) Then we can apply the steps (c)–(d). As a result
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}
is finitely generated, say by
k
non-zero elements
\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{k}
, which we may assume homogeneous.

(e
′
)

We can now deduce that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
is generated by elements
x_{i}
with leading terms the

\bar{x}_{i}
’s. Indeed, let
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
, and
[\mu]\in P_{+}^{n}
such that
x\in{\mathcal{F}}^{[\mu]}\!\setminus\!{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]}
, where
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]}:=\bigoplus_{[\mu^{\prime}]\prec[\mu]}\!C([\mu^{\prime}])
. In
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}_{[\mu]}\!=\!{\mathcal{F}}^{[\mu]}/{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]}
, we have

\bar{x}\!=\!\sum_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in I}\lambda_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})}\bar{x}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\bar{x}_{k}^{i_{k}}

for some finite set
{I\!\subset\!\mathbb{N}^{k}}
, scalars
\lambda_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
, and monomials
\bar{x}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\bar{x}_{k}^{i_{k}}
of degree
[\mu]
. By definition of the product in
{\rm Gr}_{{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}
,

\bar{x}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\bar{x}_{k}^{i_{k}}=x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{k}^{i_{k}}+{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]},

so
x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{k}^{i_{k}}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{[\mu]}\setminus{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]}
, whence
\smash{\bar{x}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\bar{x}_{k}^{i_{k}}}=\smash{\overline{x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{k}^{i_{k}}}}
and

x-\sum_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in I}\lambda_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})}x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{k}^{i_{k}}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prec[\mu]}.

The conclusion follows by decreasing induction on
[\mu]
, since at last we terminate at
{{\mathcal{F}}^{[0]}\cong\mathbb{C}(q)}
.

By combining the steps (a
′
) to (e
′
), we get that
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n}
is a Noetherian and finitely generated ring.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

(1)

Because
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}
is the center of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, (e
′
) proves it is finitely generated. Of course this follows also from the isomorphism
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\cong U_{q}^{\rm lf}
and the fact that the center of
U_{q}^{\rm lf}
is the center of
U_{q}
(by Theorem
2.1
), plus the well-known description of the latter.

(2)

In the
{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
case the filtration
\mathcal{F}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
should be related via the Wilson loop isomorphism (defined in
[
18
, Section 8.2]
) to the filtration of skein algebras of spheres with
n+1
punctures used in
[
93
]
.

4
Proof of Theorem
1.2

As usual we let
\epsilon
be a primitive
l
-th root of unity with
l
odd and
l>d_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. We now consider the specialization
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
at
q=\epsilon
, defined in Section
2.2.1
. Recall the isomorphism of algebras
\eta^{*}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
(see (
2.71
)), and that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
as a vector space. Consider the linear subspace of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
defined by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
.
This space is naturally a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
(endowed with the componentwise product
\star
). In fact, we also have the following.

Proposition 4.1
.

(1)

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a central subalgebra of the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
, and the
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-modules
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
, with actions defined by the respective products of these algebras, do coincide.

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a free
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module of rank
l^{n.\dim\mathfrak{g}}
.

(3)

\big(\eta^{*}{}^{-1}\big)^{\otimes n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(G)^{\otimes n}
is an isomorphism of algebras, and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a Noetherian ring.

(4)

The
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is finite and Noetherian. Therefore,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a Noetherian ring.

Note that the proof we give in (4) of the fact that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is Noetherian is independent from the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

(1) Let us show that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a central subalgebra of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. In the case
n=1
, the formula (
2.9
) implies that
\alpha\beta=\alpha\star\beta
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
and
\beta\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
. Indeed, by (
2.9
) we have

\displaystyle\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=\sum_{(R),(R)}(R_{(2^{\prime})}{S}(R_{(2)})\rhd\alpha)\star(R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd\beta\lhd R_{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(R),(R),(\alpha),(\beta)}\alpha_{(1)}\star(\beta_{(1)}(R_{(1)}\alpha_{(3)}(S(R_{(2)}))\beta_{(3)}(R_{(1^{\prime})}\alpha_{(2)}(R_{(2^{\prime})}))\beta_{(2)}),

where all components
\alpha_{(1)},\alpha_{(2)},\alpha_{(3)}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
, since
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
is a Hopf subalgebra of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
. But

\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\alpha_{(3)}(S(R_{(2)}))=S^{-1}(\Phi^{-}(S_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}}(\alpha_{(3)})))\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),

since
\Phi^{-}(S_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}}(\alpha_{(3)}))\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
by Theorem
2.29
(2). Similarly,
\sum_{(R)}R_{(1^{\prime})}\alpha_{(2)}(R_{(2^{\prime})})\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
. In general, these elements belong to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
and not
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm ad}\big)
because of the “diagonal” factor
\Theta
of the
R
-matrix in (
2.4
). By Lemma
2.28
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\big(U_{A}^{\rm ad}\big)
acts by the trivial character
\varepsilon
(the counit) on specializations of
\Gamma
-modules. The action of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{A})
is the counit
\varepsilon
multiplied with some powers of
\epsilon^{1/D}
.
However,
[
18
, Propositions 4.1 and 4.10]
show that such powers of
\epsilon^{1/D}
eventually disappear in the sum above; this is because the sum can be rewritten in terms of copies of the quasi
R
-matrix
\hat{R}
in (
2.4
) and the pivotal element
\ell
, instead of copies of
R
. Therefore,

\alpha\beta=\sum_{(\alpha),(\beta)}\alpha_{(1)}\star(\varepsilon(\beta_{(1)})\varepsilon(\alpha_{(3)})\varepsilon(\beta_{(3)})\varepsilon(\alpha_{(2)})\beta_{(2)})=\alpha\star\beta.

(4.1)

This shows
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
coincide as modules over

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
.
Next, we show that the subalgebras
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})^{(a)}
are central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
for all
a=1,\dots,n
. This fact will conclude the proof that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
coincide as
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-modules, because the subalgebras
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})^{(a)}
generate the space
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
in
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{\otimes n}
, and hence in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
(this follows from the comment before (
2.18
)).

In order to show that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})^{(a)}
is central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
for all
a=1,\dots,n
, it is enough to show
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})^{(a)}
commutes with the elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
supported by the tensor factors
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{(b)}
with
b\neq a
. Since
(\alpha)^{(a)}\otimes(\beta)^{(b)}=((\alpha)^{(a)}\otimes 1)(1\otimes(\beta)^{(b)})
by the definition, we have to show that
\big(1\!\otimes\!(\beta)^{(b)}\big)\big((\alpha)^{(a)}\!\otimes\!1\big)=(\alpha)^{(a)}\!\otimes\!(\beta)^{(b)}
whenever
\alpha\!\in\!\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
. We have (denoting
\sum_{(\alpha),(\alpha),(\alpha),(\alpha)}
by
\sum_{(\alpha)^{4}}
,
\Delta(\alpha_{(1)})=\sum_{(\alpha)}\alpha_{(1)(1)}\otimes\alpha_{(1)(2)}
etc.):

\displaystyle\big(1\otimes(\beta)^{(b)}\big)\big((\alpha)^{(a)}\otimes 1\big)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\scriptscriptstyle(R^{i})}\big(S\big(R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\rhd\alpha\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\big)^{(a)}

\displaystyle\otimes\big(S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)}\big)\rhd\beta\lhd R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\big)^{(b)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\scriptscriptstyle(R^{i}),(\alpha)^{4},(\beta)^{2}}(\alpha_{(2)})^{(a)}\otimes(\beta_{(2)})^{(b)}

\displaystyle\times\beta_{(1)}\big(\alpha_{(1)(2)}\big(R^{2}_{(1)}\big)R^{2}_{(2)}\alpha_{(3)(1)}\big(S\big(R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\big)R^{4}_{(2)}\big)

\displaystyle\times\beta_{(3)}\big(\alpha_{(3)(2)}\big(R^{3}_{(1)}\big)R^{3}_{(2)}\alpha_{(1)(1)}\big(R^{1}_{(1)}\big)S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}\big)\big).

By Theorem
2.29
(2), it follows that

\alpha_{(1)(2)}\big(R^{2}_{(1)}\big)R^{2}_{(2)}=\Phi^{+}(\alpha_{(1)(2)})\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),

and similarly

\alpha_{(3)(1)}\big(S\big(R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\big)R^{4}_{(2)},\alpha_{(3)(2)}\big(R^{3}_{(1)}\big)R^{3}_{(2)},\alpha_{(1)(1)}\big(R^{1}_{(1)}\big)S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}\big)\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}).

Denote by
z
any such element;
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm ad}\big)
acts by the trivial character (the counit
\varepsilon
) on specializations of
\Gamma
-modules. By using
[
18
, Proposition 6.2]
, arguing as above (
4.1
), we obtain that the expression of
z
in terms of the corresponding
\alpha_{(i)(j)}
involves
\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon(\alpha_{(i)(j)})
only
(
no root
\epsilon^{1/D}
)
. It follows

\displaystyle\beta_{(1)}\big(\alpha_{(1)(2)}\big(R^{2}_{(1)}\big)R^{2}_{(2)}\alpha_{(3)(1)}\big(S\big(R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\big)R^{4}_{(2)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\varepsilon(\alpha_{(1)(2)}\alpha_{(3)(1)})\beta_{(1)}(1)=\varepsilon(\alpha_{(1)(2)})\varepsilon(\alpha_{(3)(1)})\varepsilon(\beta_{(1)}),

\displaystyle\beta_{(3)}\big(\alpha_{(3)(2)}\big(R^{3}_{(1)}\big)R^{3}_{(2)}\alpha_{(1)(1)}\big(R^{1}_{(1)}\big)S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}\big)\big)=\varepsilon(\alpha_{(3)(2)})\varepsilon(\alpha_{(1)(1)})\varepsilon(\beta_{(3)}).

Therefore,
\big(1\otimes(\beta)^{(b)}\big)\big((\alpha)^{(a)}\otimes 1\big)=(\alpha)^{(a)}\otimes(\beta)^{(b)}
. It follows that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
as modules over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
; for instance when
n=2
, given
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
we have
(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\beta^{\prime})(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes 1)(1\otimes\beta^{\prime})(\alpha\otimes 1)(1\otimes\beta)
immediately by (
2.17
), and
(1\otimes\beta^{\prime})(\alpha\otimes 1)=\alpha\otimes\beta^{\prime}=(\alpha\otimes 1)(1\otimes\beta^{\prime})
as above. Then
(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\beta^{\prime})(\alpha\otimes\beta)=\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\otimes\beta^{\prime}\beta
. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a central subalgebra of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
.

(2) Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
coincide as modules over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\big)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n})
, the claim follows from Theorem
2.29
, that is, from
[
41
, Theorem 7.2]
, which shows that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
is a finitely generated projective module of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
, and from the arguments of
[
28
]
(using that
K_{0}(\mathcal{O}(G))=\mathbb{Z}
by
[
87
]
), which imply that this module is free. Alternatively, it follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
is a cleft extension of
{\mathcal{O}}(G)
(see
[
6
, Remark 2.18 (b)]
, and
[
25
, Section 3.2]
).

(3) The linear isomorphism
\big(\eta^{*}{}^{-1}\big)^{\otimes n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(G)^{\otimes n}
is an isomorphism of algebras because
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. It implies that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a Noetherian ring, since
{\mathcal{O}}(G)^{\otimes n}={\mathcal{O}}(G^{n})
and
G^{n}
is an affine algebraic variety.

(4) The fact that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module follows from (2); an alternative proof of this fact will be provided at the end of the proof of Theorem
4.9
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is finite over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is Noetherian,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a Noetherian
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module (e.g., by
[
7
, Proposition 6.5]
). It follows that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a Noetherian ring (e.g., by
[
88
, Chapter 1, Section 1.3]
).∎

Recall that we denote
U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}=U_{A}^{\rm lf}\bigotimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
(see (
2.27
)), and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\subset U_{\epsilon}
is the central polynomial subalgebra generated by
E_{\beta_{k}}^{l}
,
F_{\beta_{k}}^{l}
,
L_{i}^{\pm l}
, for
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Since
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow U^{\rm lf}_{\epsilon}
is an embedding of algebras (see Corollary
2.25
), it identifies
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
with a central subalgebra of
U^{\rm lf}_{\epsilon}
. Put
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U^{\rm lf}_{\epsilon}\big):=\Phi_{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr))
.
Recall Theorem
2.1
, Proposition
2.24
, and let
T^{(l)}
,
T_{2-}^{(l)}
and
T_{2}^{(l)}
be the subsets of
T
,
T_{2-}
and
T_{2}
formed by the elements
K_{\lambda l}
with
\lambda\in P
,
\lambda\in-2P_{+}
and
\lambda\in 2P
, respectively.

Proposition 4.2
.

We have
U_{\epsilon}=T_{2-}^{-1}U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}[T/T_{2}]=\Phi_{1}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-1}\big]\big)[T/T_{2}]
, and therefore the map
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-1}\big]\rightarrow T_{2-}^{-1}U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}
is an isomorphism.

Moreover,
\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)=U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\cap\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
, and

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})=T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big],\qquad\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})=T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big].

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from Proposition
2.24
by specialization at
{q=\epsilon}
. For the second claim, the inclusion
U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\cap\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
is clear, and for the converse inclusion we only have to show that the elements of
\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
commute with
T
. They commute with
T_{2}\subset U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}
, so the conjugation action by elements of
T
on
\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
has order at most
2
. Let
x\in\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
with decomposition
x=\sum_{i}c_{i}x_{i}
with all
c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
and
x_{i}
PBW basis vectors, and let
\lambda\in P
. We have
{K_{\lambda}xK_{-\lambda}=\sum_{i}c_{i}q(x_{i})x_{i}}
, where
q(x_{i})\in\epsilon^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
q(x_{i})^{2}=1
for all
i
. Because
\epsilon
has odd order the only possibility is
q(x_{i})=1
, whence
K_{\lambda}xK_{-\lambda}=x
. The conclusion follows.

The inclusion
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
follows from the definition
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
, the formula
{\Phi_{1}=m\circ\big({\rm id}\otimes S^{-1}\big)\circ\Phi}
, and the fact that
\Phi
affords an embedding
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(G^{*}))
(see Theorem
2.29
(2)). Since
T^{(l)}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
, we obtain

T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big]\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}).

The proof of the converse inclusion is similar to that in Proposition
2.24
. The isomorphism
\smash{\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})\big[\psi_{-l\rho}^{-1}\big]}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(G^{*}))
of Theorem
2.29
(2) implies

F_{\beta_{k}}^{l}K_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\otimes 1,1\otimes K_{\beta_{k}}^{-l}E_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\in\Phi\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})\big[\psi_{-l\rho}^{-1}\big]\big)

for every positive root
\beta_{k}
. Since
\psi_{-l\rho}=\Phi_{1}^{-1}(K_{-2l\rho})=\psi_{-\rho}^{l}
(the
l
-th power of
\psi_{-\rho}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
), and

\Phi_{1}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[\psi_{-\rho}^{-l}\big]\big)=T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big),

it follows that

F_{\beta_{k}}^{l}K_{\beta_{k}}^{l},S^{-1}\bigl(E_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\bigr)K_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\in T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big).

Hence
F_{\beta_{k}}^{l},S^{-1}\big(E_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\big)\in T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big]
. The sets
S^{-1}\big(E_{\beta_{k}}^{l}\big)\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h}))
,
k=1,\dots,N
, generate the subalgebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
, so from the triangular decomposition
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}_{-}))\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h}))\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}_{+}))
this proves the inclusion
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\subset T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big]
.
From the isomorphism

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon})\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})

(see Theorem
2.27
), and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}(U_{q})\subset U_{q}^{\rm lf}

(
whence
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
)
, the equality
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})=T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big]
follows at once.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Let us explain how this can be used to give an interpretation of the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)
. Recall the notations introduced around Theorem
2.27
. Since
G^{*}=U_{+}T_{G}U_{-}
, we have
{\mathcal{O}}(G^{*})={\mathcal{O}}(U_{+}){\mathcal{O}}(T_{G}){\mathcal{O}}(U_{-})
, and the map
\sigma
yields an identification

{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)={\mathcal{O}}(U_{+}){\mathcal{O}}(T_{G}/(2)){\mathcal{O}}(U_{-}).

(4.2)

We can identify
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)
with the subalgebra
(\sigma_{|G^{*}})^{*}\big({\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)\big)\subset{\mathcal{O}}(G^{*})
. Consider the exterior power
V=\wedge^{N}\mathfrak{g}
endowed with the action
\wedge^{N}{\rm Ad}
of
G
. Put on
\mathfrak{g}
a basis consisting of one element
e_{\alpha}
per root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, along with a basis of
\mathfrak{h}
. Let
v\in V
be the exterior power of the
e_{\alpha}
’s for
\alpha
negative, and
v^{*}
a dual vector such that
v^{*}(v)=1
and
v^{*}
vanishes on a
T_{G}
-invariant complement of
v
. It is classical that
G\setminus G^{0}
has defining equation
{\delta(g)=0}
, where
\delta
is the matrix coefficient
\delta(g)=v^{*}(\pi_{V}(g)v)
(see, e.g.,
[
59
, p. 174]
). Hence
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)={\mathcal{O}}(G)\big[\delta^{-1}\big]
. On
G^{0}
we have
\delta(u_{+}tu_{-})=\chi_{-2\rho}(t)
, where
\chi_{-2\rho}
is the character of
T_{G}
associated to the root
-2\rho
. Now we can make the connection with
U_{\epsilon}
. The isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G^{*})
of Theorem
2.27
(2) identifies
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h}))=\mathbb{C}\big[T^{(l)}\big]
with
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{G})
by mapping
K_{\lambda l}
to the character of
T_{G}
associated to
\lambda
. Therefore, it maps
\mathbb{C}\big[T_{2}^{(l)}\big]
to
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{G}/(2))
, and
T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
to
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)
by (
4.2
) and Proposition
4.2
. Since
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)={\mathcal{O}}(G)\big[\delta^{-1}\big]
and
T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[\ell^{l}\big]
, it follows that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(G)
coincide after localization by
\ell^{l}
and
\delta
respectively. By using the Bruhat decomposition of
G
as in (
4.6
) in the proof of Theorem
4.9
below, one can deduce
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)
, whence
{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)}
by injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
.

Let us make the following observation. Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\bigotimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
, with
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}={\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{\otimes n}
as an
A
-module, and a generating system of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{\otimes n}
is also a generating system of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, it follows from Proposition
2.10
and the identities (
2.56
)–(
2.57
) that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is generated by elements of the form
{\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n}}
, where
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
belong to the set
C_{\rm gen}
of matrix coefficients lying on the first and last columns of the matrix representations of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
in the canonical bases of the modules
{{}_{A}V_{\varpi_{i}}}
,
i=1,\dots,m
. Denote by
\alpha^{\star k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th power of an element
{\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A}}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For all
\alpha\in C_{\rm gen}
,
\alpha^{\star l}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
.

Proof.

Recall that the Frobenius epimorphism
\eta\colon U_{A}^{\rm res}\bigotimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow U(\mathfrak{g})
in (
2.71
) has kernel the ideal
I
generated by the elements
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
K_{i}-1
, and
(K_{i};p)_{q_{i}}
where
l
does not divide
p
,
{i=1,\dots,m}
. It follows that an element of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
belongs to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})=\eta^{*}({\mathcal{O}}(G))
if and only if it vanishes on
I
. But this is immediate to check for the elements of the form
\alpha^{\star l}
with
\alpha\in C_{\rm gen}
, using that
K_{i}
is grouplike and the pure summands of
\Delta(E_{i})
and
\Delta(F_{i})
have one component equal to
1
or
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
and the other component equal to
E_{i}
or
F_{i}
. For instance,

\psi_{\varpi_{i}}^{\star l}(K_{i}-1)=\psi_{\varpi_{i}}(K_{i})^{l}-1=\epsilon^{l(\alpha_{i},\varpi_{i})}-1=0.

Similarly, for every
\alpha\in C_{\rm gen}
, we find

\alpha^{\star l}(E_{i})=\alpha^{\otimes l}\big(\Delta^{(l)}(E_{i})\big)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha^{\star l}(F_{i})=\alpha^{\star l}(K_{i}-1)=0.

We need below explicit descriptions of the centers of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2})
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
and their
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
-subalgebras. Denote by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
the standard generators of
\mathcal{O}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})
, i.e., the matrix coefficients in the basis of weight vectors
v_{0}
,
v_{1}=F.v_{0}
of the
2
-dimensional irreducible representation
V_{1}
of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
. As above, denote by
x^{\star k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th power of an element
x\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})
. The algebra
\mathcal{O}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})
is generated by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
; the monomials
a^{\star i}\star b^{\star j}\star d^{\star r}
and
a^{\star i}\star c^{\star k}\star d^{\star r}
,
i,j,k,r\in\mathbb{N},k>0
, form an
A
-basis of
\mathcal{O}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})
. The algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
is generated by
a^{\star l}
,
b^{\star l}
,
c^{\star l}
,
d^{\star l}
; the monomials
a^{\star il}\star b^{\star jl}\star d^{\star rl}
and
a^{\star il}\star c^{\star kl}\star d^{\star rl}
form a basis of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
, and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
is generated by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
and the elements
b^{\star(l-k)}\star c^{\star k}
,
k=0,\dots,l
(see
[
41
, Proposition 1.4 and the appendix]
). We have the relation

a^{\star l}\star d^{\star l}-b^{\star l}\star c^{\star l}=1

(4.3)

and the Frobenius isomorphism of Parshall–Wang (see
[
92
, Chapter 7]
) coincides with the map

{\rm Fr}_{\rm PW}\colon\ {\mathcal{O}}({\rm SL}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))

induced by
\eta^{*}
; it sends the standard generators
\underline{a}
,
\underline{b}
,
\underline{c}
,
\underline{d}
of
{\mathcal{O}}({\rm SL}_{2})=\mathcal{O}_{1}({\rm SL}_{2})
respectively to
a^{\star l}
,
b^{\star l}
,
c^{\star l}
,
d^{\star l}
. Finally, we have seen that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2})
is a free
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}({\rm SL}_{2}))
-module of rank
l^{3}
(see Theorem
2.29
(3)). In
[
38
]
, it is shown that a basis of this module is formed by the monomials
a^{m}b^{n}c^{s^{\prime}}
and
b^{n}c^{s^{\prime\prime}}d^{r}
, with the integers
m
,
n
,
r
,
s^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime\prime}
in the range

1\leq m\leq l-1,\qquad 0\leq n,r\leq l-1,\qquad m\leq s^{\prime}\leq l-1,\qquad 0\leq s^{\prime\prime}\leq l-r-1.

(4.4)

Now consider
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
. Recall that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}={\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as
U_{A}
-modules. The algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
is also generated by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
; a set of defining relations is (see
[
18
, Section 5]
):

\displaystyle ad=da,\qquad db=q^{2}bd,\qquad cd=q^{2}dc,\qquad ab-ba=-\big(1-q^{-2}\big)bd,

\displaystyle cb-bc=\big(1-q^{-2}\big)\big(da-d^{2}\big),\qquad ac-ca=\big(1-q^{-2}\big)dc,\qquad ad-q^{2}bc=1.

(4.5)

The element
\omega:=qa+q^{-1}d
is central. Let
T_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, be such that
T_{k}(x)/2
is the
k
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first type in the variable
x/2
. We have (see
[
18
, Proposition 7.2]
, for the generalization to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
):

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the ideal of
\mathbb{C}\big[\omega,b^{l},c^{l},d^{l}\big]
generated by
\big(T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l}\big)d^{l}-b^{l}c^{l}-1
, we have

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))=\mathbb{C}\big[\omega,b^{l},c^{l},d^{l}\big]/\mathcal{I}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))=\mathbb{C}\big[T_{l}(\omega),b^{l},c^{l},d^{l}\big]/\mathcal{I}.

Here
b^{l}
,
c^{l}
,
d^{l}
are the
l
-th powers of
b
,
c
,
d
computed using the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
, not the product
\star
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
. The above generator of
\mathcal{I}
can be interpreted as a determinant, and
\omega
as a quantum trace on
V_{1}
. The following has also been observed in
[
75
]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Viewed as elements of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})
,
T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l}=a^{\star l}
and
x^{l}=x^{\star l}
,
x\in\{b,c,d\}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
and
\varpi
be the simple root and fundamental weight of
{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
. In the notations of (
2.70
), we have
b=\psi^{-\alpha}_{-\varpi}
,
c=\psi^{\alpha}_{-\varpi}
,
d=\psi_{-\varpi}
; the formulas of
\Phi
give

\Phi_{1}\big(b^{\star l}\big)=\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l},\qquad\Phi_{1}\big(c^{\star l}\big)=\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{l}E^{l}K^{-l},\qquad\Phi_{1}\big(d^{\star l}\big)=K^{-l}.

These coincide respectively with
\Phi_{1}\big(b^{l}\big)
,
\Phi_{1}(c^{l})
,
\Phi_{1}\big(d^{l}\big)
(see
[
18
, equation (5.3)]
). By passing to the localization
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})\big[d^{-1}\big]
, and using Parshall–Wang’s relation (
4.3
), one deduces easily

\Phi_{1}\big(a^{\star l}\big)=K^{l}+\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{2l}F^{l}E^{l}=T_{l}(\Omega)-K^{-l},

where
\Omega=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2}FE+\epsilon K+\epsilon^{-1}K^{-1}
is the Casimir element, and
T_{l}(x)/2
is the
l
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first type in the variable
x/2
. We have
\Phi_{1}(\omega)=\Omega
, so
\Phi_{1}\big(a^{\star l}\big)=T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l}
. The conclusion follows from the injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
.
∎

This lemma proves that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}({\rm SL}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Fr}_{\rm PW}\ \ \ }
\scriptstyle{\rm Fr}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad\quad\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}),}

where
{\rm Fr}_{\rm PW}
is Parshall–Wang’s Frobenius isomorphism recalled above,
{\rm Fr}
is the Frobenius isomorphism introduced in
[
18
, Definition 7.1]
, and the vertical arrows are the identifications as vector spaces (the middle one proved by Proposition
4.1
).

Remark 4.7
.

By Lemma
4.5
, the monomials
T_{l}(\omega)^{i}b^{jl}d^{rl}
and
T_{l}(\omega)^{i}c^{kl}d^{rl}
, for
i,j,k,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
{k>0}
, form an
A
-basis of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))
. It is straightforward (though cumbersome) to express these basis elements in terms of the basis elements
a^{\star il}\star b^{\star jl}\star d^{\star rl}
and
a^{\star il}\star c^{\star kl}\star d^{\star rl}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
, and conversely; this can be done by using Lemma
4.6
, the formula (
2.9
) or the inverse one (expressing
\star
in terms of the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, see
[
18
, equation (4.6)]
), and the formula of the coproduct
\Delta\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))
restricted to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))
(given in
[
18
, Lemma 7.5]
).

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}={\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as an
A
-module, the functionals
t_{i}
in Proposition
2.30
can be seen as maps
t_{i}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow A
. Though the algebra structures of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
are very different,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
satisfies a result analogous to Proposition
2.30
:

Proposition 4.8
.

The maps
\lhd t_{i}
preserve
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, and they satisfy
(f\lhd t_{i})(a)=f(n_{i}a)
and
{(f\alpha)\lhd t_{i}=(f\lhd t_{i})(\alpha\lhd t_{i})}
for every
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
,
a\in G
,
\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The first two claims follow from Proposition
2.30
and the definition
{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})}
.

The last claim follows from the case
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
, as in the proof of
[
41
, Proposition 7.1]
. In fact, it is enough to show that
t(fg)=t(f)t(g)
for every
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
,
g\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
; for completeness we explain this in Appendix
C
, see (
C.3
). A word of caution is needed: the proof of (
C.3
) uses that
\Delta\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
is a morphism of algebras. The analogous property for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is that
\Delta
yields a morphism of algebras
\Delta\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}^{\epsilon}
. Since the algebra structure of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}^{\epsilon}
is not the product one on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
, it is not true in general that

\sum_{(f),(g)}(f_{(1)}\otimes f_{(2)})(g_{(1)}\otimes g_{(2)})=\sum_{(f),(g)}f_{(1)}g_{(1)}\otimes f_{(2)}g_{(2)}

for every
f,g\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
. However, it holds whenever
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, since
\Delta(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr))\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
and therefore
f_{(2)}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
commutes in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}^{\epsilon}
with any
{g_{(1)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}}
.

It is enough to prove the identity
t(fg)=t(f)t(g)
when
f
ranges in a set of generators of the algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}))
. So one can take
f
among, say,
T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l}=a^{\star l}
and
x^{l}=x^{\star l}
,
x\in\{b,c,d\}
(using Lemma
4.5
). By (
2.9
) and Proposition
C.1
, we have

t(fg)=\sum_{(R),(R)}t\left(R_{(2^{\prime})}{S}(R_{(2)})\rhd f\right)t\left(R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd g\lhd R_{(1)}\right).

Expanding coproducts and using that
R^{-1}=(S\otimes{\rm id})(R)
, we deduce

\displaystyle t(fg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f),(R),(R)}t\big(f_{(1)}\big)\left\langle f_{(2)},R_{(2^{\prime})}{S}(R_{(2)})\right\rangle t\left(R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd g\lhd R_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f),(R),(R)}t\big(f_{(1)}\big)t(\left\langle f_{(2)},R_{(2^{\prime})}\right\rangle R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd g\lhd\left\langle f_{(3)},S(R_{(2)})\right\rangle R_{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f)}t(f_{(1)})t\big(S^{-1}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(2)}))\rhd g\lhd S^{-2}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(3)}))\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f)}t(f_{(1)})\big\langle g,S^{-2}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(3)}))\underline{w}S^{-1}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(2)}))\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f)}t(f_{(1)})\varepsilon\big(S^{-2}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(3)}))\big)\varepsilon\big(S^{-1}(\Phi^{-}(f_{(2)}))\big)t(g),

where
\underline{w}\in\mathbb{U}_{\Gamma}
is the quantum Weyl group element dual to
t
(see Appendix
B
), and in the last equality we used that
\Phi^{-}
maps
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
into
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
(see Theorem
2.29
(2)), which acts on specializations of
\Gamma
-modules by the trivial character (the counit)
\varepsilon\colon U_{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
By (
B.6
)–(
B.7
)
, we have
t\big(a^{\star l}\big)=t\big(d^{\star l}\big)=0
and
t\big(b^{\star l}\big)=1
,
t\big(c^{\star l}\big)=-1
. Now the computation of
t(fg)
follows easily. For instance, taking
f=b^{l}=b^{\star l}
, by using
\Delta\big(b^{\star l}\big)=a^{\star l}\otimes b^{\star l}+b^{\star l}\otimes d^{\star l}
and
\Delta\big(d^{\star l}\big)=c^{\star l}\otimes b^{\star l}+d^{\star l}\otimes d^{\star l}
, we get

\displaystyle t(b^{l}g)={}

\displaystyle\varepsilon\big(S^{-2}\big(\Phi^{-}\big(b^{\star l}\big)\big)\big)\varepsilon\big(S^{-1}\big(\Phi^{-}\big(c^{\star l}\big)\big)\big)t(g)+\varepsilon\big(S^{-2}\big(\Phi^{-}\big(d^{\star l}\big)\big)\big)\varepsilon\big(S^{-1}\big(\Phi^{-}\big(d^{\star l}\big)\big)\big)t(g).

Since
b^{\star l}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}(U_{+})
,
\Phi^{-}\big(b^{\star l}\big)=0
. Also, it is immediate from the definition of
\Phi^{-}
that
\Phi^{-}\big(d^{\star l}\big)=\Phi^{-}(d)^{l}=L^{l}
; alternatively, one can bypass this computation by observing that
\Phi^{-}
sets an isomorphism from
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}(T_{G})={\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}(B_{+})\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}(B_{-})
to
\mathbb{C}\big[L^{\pm 1}\big]=U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\cap U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})
, mapping a generator
d
to
L
or
L^{-1}
. We have
\varepsilon\big(L^{l}\big)=1
, and therefore

\displaystyle t\big(b^{l}g\big)=t(g)=t\big(b^{l}\big)t(g).

The other cases
f=T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l},c^{l},d^{l}
are similar.
∎

Theorem 4.9
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a free
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module of rank
l^{n.\dim\mathfrak{g}}
, and
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
is a Noetherian ring and a finite, whence Noetherian,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module.

Proof.

We already proved the first claim in Proposition
4.1
, and that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a Noetherian
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module. For the second claim, it follows that the
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-submodule
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
is necessarily finitely generated. But
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
being Noetherian,
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
is then a Noetherian
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module and a Noetherian ring.

For the sake of clarity, let us provide a self-contained proof of the first claim, not invoking directly
[
28
,
41
]
or
[
6
,
25
]
, but applying the same arguments directly to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}
coincide as modules over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{\otimes n}
by Proposition
4.1
, the result will follow from the case
n=1
. Then we argue in four steps. First, using Theorem
2.1
we show that a certain localization of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is a free module of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
. Then, assuming that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and projective, we explain why it has constant rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
(this is very classical). Thirdly, we prove that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and projective as in
[
41
, Theorem 7.2]
. Finally, we obtain that it is a free module as in
[
28
]
.

Recall Proposition
4.2
:
U_{\epsilon}
is a free
\Phi_{1}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]\big)
-module of rank
2^{m}

(
note that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-1}\big]
)
,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is free over

T_{2-}^{(l)-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)=\Phi_{1}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\big)

of rank
2^{m}
. Since
U_{\epsilon}
is also free of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
(see Theorem
2.27
(1)), it is free over
\Phi_{1}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\big)
of rank
2^{m}l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
. The decomposition being unique,
\Phi_{1}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]\big)
is free of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
\Phi_{1}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\big)
, and injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
implies that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]
is free of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
.

Assume now that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and projective. Let us show that its rank is
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
. The localization
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)_{P}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
at any prime ideal
P
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a free module over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)_{P}

[
96
, Proposition 2.12.15]
; the ranks of such modules are finite in number
[
96
, Proposition 2.12.20]
. If these ranks are all equal, then, by definition, it is the rank of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. This happens if
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
has no nontrivial (i.e.,
\neq 1
) idempotent
[
96
, Corollary 2.12.23]
, which is the case since it has no nontrivial zero divisors. To compute the rank, suppose
P
does not contain
d^{l}=\psi_{-\rho}^{l}
. Such ideals
P
are in
1
-
1
correspondence with the prime ideals of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
by the natural ring monomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
. The set
{S=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\setminus P}
is multiplicatively closed, and we have also a ring morphism
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\rightarrow S^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, which is also an injection (there are no zero divisors in
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, whence in
S
). Then

\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)_{P}=S^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)[d^{-l}]}S^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)

shows that
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)_{P}
has over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)_{P}=S^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
the same rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
as
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
. This proves our claim.

In order to show that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and projective over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, it is enough to show it is finite locally free, i.e., there are elements
d_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
such that the localizations
{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d_{i}^{-1}\big]}
are finite free
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d_{i}^{-1}\big]
-modules, and
{\rm Maxspec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigl)
is covered by the open sets
U(d_{i})
made of the ideals not containing
d_{i}
(see
[
100
, Lemma 77.2]
).

We have seen above that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]
is free of rank
l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
. By Remark
4.3
,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\cong\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big[\ell^{l}\big]
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)
, and
{\mathcal{O}}\big(G^{0}\big)={\mathcal{O}}(G)\big[\delta^{-1}\big]
. Now, given
w\in W
with a reduced expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
, put
t_{w}=t_{i_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k}}
. Let
w
be represented by
n_{w}=n_{i_{1}}\cdots n_{i_{k}}
in
N(T_{G})
. By Proposition
4.8
, we have
(f\lhd t_{w})(x)=f(n_{w}x)
for every
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
,
x\in G
. Then

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]\lhd t_{w}\cong{\mathcal{O}}\big(n_{w}^{-1}G^{0}\big)\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)\big[(\delta\lhd t_{w})^{-1}\big].

(4.6)

If
b_{1},\dots,b_{r}

(
r:=l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
)
is a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]
over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d^{-l}\big]
, then
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d^{-l}\big]\lhd t_{w}
is free over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[(d\lhd t_{w})^{-l}\big]\cong{\mathcal{O}}\big(n_{w}^{-1}G^{0}\big)
with basis
b_{1}\lhd t_{w},\dots,b_{r}\lhd t_{w}
. Consider the Bruhat decomposition of
G
: any
g\in G
can be written in the form
g=b_{1}nb_{2}
, where
b_{1},b_{2}\in B_{-}
,
n\in W
. Hence
g=nn^{-1}b_{1}nb_{2}\in nB_{+}B_{-}=nG^{0}
, and therefore

G=\bigcup_{w\in W}(B_{-}n_{w}B_{-})=\bigcup_{w\in W}\big(n_{w}G^{0}\big).

For every
w\in W
, put
d^{l}_{w}:=d^{l}\lhd t_{w}
.
Under the isomorphism of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
with
{\mathcal{O}}(G)
, we thus get that
{\rm Maxspec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
is covered by the open sets
U\big(d^{l}_{w}\big)\cong n_{w}G^{0}
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\big[d_{w}^{-l}\big]
is finite free over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[d_{w}^{-l}\big]
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and projective over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
.

Finally, let us explain why
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is free over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, following the arguments of
[
28
]
. Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian ring, put
X={\rm Maxspec}(R)
, and let
P
be an
R
-module. Denote by
R_{I}
,
P_{I}
the localizations of
R
,
P
at a maximal ideal
I\in X
. Define the
f-rank
of
P
as f-rank
(P)=\inf_{I\in X}\{
f-rank
{}_{R_{I}}(P_{I})\}
, where f-rank
{{}_{R_{I}}(P_{I})=\sup\bigl\{r\in\mathbb{N},R_{I}^{\otimes r}\subset P_{I}\bigr\}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}}
(i.e., the maximal dimension of a free summand of
P_{I}
). Bass’ Cancellation theorem asserts that if
P
is projective and f-rank
(P)>{\rm dim}(X)
, and
P\oplus Q\cong M\oplus Q
for some
R
-modules
Q
and
M
such that
Q
is finitely generated and projective, then
P\cong M
(see
[
19
, Section IV.3.5, p. 167 and p. 170]
, taking
{A=R}
, or
[
88
, Section 11.7.13]
). Let us apply this to
{R={\mathcal{O}}(G)}
and
P={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
. We proved above that f-rank
{}_{R_{I}}(P_{I})=l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}
, a constant, and we have
{l^{\dim\mathfrak{g}}>\dim\mathfrak{g}=\dim(G)}
. By a result of Marlin
[
87
]
,
G
being semisimple and simply connected the Grothendieck ring
K_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(G)\!)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\oplus Q\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)^{r}
for some free
{\mathcal{O}}(G)
-module
Q
and
{r\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then Bass’ cancellation implies
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is free over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})\cong{\mathcal{O}}(G)
.
∎

5
Proof of Theorem
1.3

We begin with the following lemma, interesting by itself.

Lemma 5.1
.

\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a finite
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module and a Noetherian ring. Therefore, the ring
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is integral over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
.

Proof.

We know by Proposition
4.1
that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a Noetherian ring, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is a finite Noetherian
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module. Therefore, the submodule
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is finitely generated. Being finite over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, it is necessarily a Noetherian ring (e.g., by
[
7
, Proposition 7.2]
).

Let
x\in\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. The
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-submodule
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)[x]
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated by the same argument. Using the fact that an element
x
is integral over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
if and only if
{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)[x]}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
-module (e.g., by
[
7
, Proposition 5.1]
), this proves the last claim.
∎

We will use the following notations. Let
A
be a ring with no nontrivial zero divisors. The center
Z=Z(A)
is a commutative integral domain. We denote by
Q(Z)
its field of fractions, and put

Q(A):=Q(Z)\bigotimes_{Z}A.

It is an algebra over its center
Q(Z)
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
has no nontrivial zero divisors
[
18
, Proposition 6.30]
, we can take
A={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
, or
A=\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
.

By the lemma,
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is finite over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, so the ring
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
is a field. Necessarily it coincides with
Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr))
, and therefore

Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}.

(5.1)

Recall that we denote by
N
the number of positive roots of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a division algebra and a central simple algebra of PI degree
l^{Nn}
.

Proof.

It follows from (
5.1
) and Theorem
4.9
that
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a vector space of dimension
l^{n.\dim\mathfrak{g}}
over
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, and therefore has finite dimension over its center
Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr))
. Because
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
has no nontrivial divisors
[
18
, Proposition 6.30]
and
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is finite-dimensional over
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
,
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is a division algebra, whence a central simple algebra. By classical theory (see, e.g.,
[
88
, Section 13.3.5]
, or
[
96
, Corollary 2.3.25]
), there is a finite extension
\mathbb{F}
of
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, a
splitting field
, such that

\mathbb{F}\bigotimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))}Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=M_{d}(\mathbb{F}),

where
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the PI degree of
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, satisfies

d^{2}=\bigl[Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]=\frac{[Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)]}{\bigl[Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]}.

(5.2)

We have to show
d^{2}=l^{2nN}
. We will obtain this equality by proving firstly that
d^{2}\geq l^{2nN}
, and then
d^{2}\leq l^{2nN}
.

In order to show that
d^{2}\geq l^{2nN}
, it is enough to exhibit an irreducible representation
V
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
of dimension
k:=l^{nN}
. Indeed, the representation map
\rho_{V}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\rm End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
being surjective, given basis elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{2}}\in{\rm End}(V)
, and elements
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k^{2}}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
such that
{\rho(\alpha_{i})=v_{i}}
for every
i\in\bigl\{1,\dots,k^{2}\bigr\}
, necessarily
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k^{2}}
form a free family of
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. For, if there was a nontrivial relation
\sum_{i}z_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
, with
z_{i}\in Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, by clearing denominators and then applying the representation map
\rho_{V}
, we would get a nontrivial relation in
{\rm End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
between
{v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{2}}}
.

Now, by Theorem
2.27
(1) (see
[
42
, Section 20]
), the dimension of a generic irreducible representation space of
U_{\epsilon}
is
l^{N}
. Because
U_{\epsilon}=T_{2-}^{-1}U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}[T/T_{2}]
by Proposition
4.2
, an irreducible representation of
U_{\epsilon}
yields an irreducible representation of
U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}
. Moreover, the tensor product of
n
irreducible representation spaces of
U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}
of dimension
l^{N}
is an irreducible representation space of
\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)^{\otimes n}
of dimension
l^{nN}
(see, e.g.,
[
51
, Theorem 3.10.2]
). Applying the linear isomorphism
\psi_{n}=\Phi_{n}\circ\big(\Phi_{1}^{-1}\big)^{\otimes n}
in (
2.21
) thus provides an irreducible representation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
of dimension
l^{nN}
.

It remains to show
d^{2}\leq l^{2nN}
, which by
\bigl[Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{n(2N+m)}
is equivalent to
{\bigl[Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]\geq l^{mn}}
. For this, it is enough to exhibit an extension of
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
contained in
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
and of degree
l^{mn}
. There is a very natural one, which we denote by
Q\big(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
and is constructed as follows. Consider for every
\lambda\in P_{+}
the matrices

M_{\lambda}:=\bigl({}_{{}_{A}V_{\lambda}}\phi_{e_{k}}^{e_{l}}\bigr)_{k,l}\in{\rm End}({}_{A}V_{\lambda})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A},\qquad M_{\lambda}^{(i)}:=\big(\bigl({}_{{}_{A}V_{\lambda}}\phi_{e_{k}}^{e_{l}}\bigr)^{(i)}\big)_{k,l}\in{\rm End}({}_{A}V_{\lambda})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A},

where
i=1,\dots,n
, and as usual
{}_{{}_{A}V_{\lambda}}\phi_{e_{k}}^{e_{l}}
is a matrix coefficient of
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
,
\{e_{k}\}
the canonical basis of
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
, and
\bigl({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi_{e_{k}}^{e_{l}}\bigr)^{(i)}:=1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi_{e_{k}}^{e_{l}}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}
. Set

{}_{\lambda}\omega:=Tr(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)M_{\lambda}),\qquad{}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)}:=Tr\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)M_{\lambda}^{(i)}\big),

where
Tr
is the standard trace on
{\rm End}(V_{\lambda})
. Clearly,
{}_{\lambda}\omega\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
,
{}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
. By
[
18
, Propositions 4.8 and 6.24]
, the family of elements
\prod_{i=1}^{n}{}_{\lambda_{i}}\omega^{(i)}
, where
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
, is a basis of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
; moreover the Alekseev map
\Phi_{n}
affords an isomorphism from
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
to
\mathcal{Z}(U_{q})^{\otimes n}
, and
\Phi_{n}\big({}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)}\big)=(\Phi_{1}({}_{\lambda}\omega))^{(i)}
. For
n=1
, specializing
q
to
\epsilon
it follows

\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon})={\rm Vect}\{\Phi_{1}({}_{\lambda}\omega),\,\lambda\in P_{+}\},

(5.3)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon})
is defined before Theorem
2.27
. Then, for every
i=1,\dots,n
define

\mathcal{Z}_{0,(i)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big[\big\{{}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)},\lambda\in P_{+}\big\}\big]

and let
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
be the algebra generated by
\mathcal{Z}_{0,(1)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr),\dots,\mathcal{Z}_{0,(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. The fields
{Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0,(i)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)}
are
n
linearly disjoint extensions of
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, so

\big[Q\big(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0,(i)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr].

Now, by Proposition
4.2
, we know that
\Phi_{1}
affords isomorphisms
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\cong Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big)
and
Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr))\cong Q\big(\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big)
, and moreover

Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))=Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big)\big(T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big),\qquad Q(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))=Q\big(\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)\big)\big(T^{(l)}/T_{2}^{(l)}\big).

(5.4)

Computing via the field embedding
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\colon Q\big(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)\rightarrow Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl(U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}\bigr)\bigr)
, we deduce

\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0,(i)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl[\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0,(i)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr):\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad=\big[Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)^{\otimes n}\big)\big[\big\{(\Phi_{1}({}_{\lambda}\omega))^{(i)},\,\lambda\in P_{+},\,i=1,\dots,n\big\}\big]:Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm lf}\big)^{\otimes n}\big)\big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\big[Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\big)\big[\big\{(\Phi_{1}({}_{\lambda}\omega))^{(i)},\,\lambda\in P_{+},\,i=1,\dots,n\big\}\big]:Q\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\big)\big]=l^{m}.

The second and third equalities follow from (
5.4
) and the properties of
\Phi_{1}
recalled before it, and the last equality follows from Theorem
2.29
(2) and (
5.3
). As a result, we have

\big[Q\big(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]=l^{mn},

whence

\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]\geq l^{mn}.

Since
\bigl[Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{n(m+2N)}
, by (
5.2
) we obtain
d^{2}\leq l^{2nN}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

It follows
\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{mn}
by the degree computation above, whence
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)=Q\big(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
. In
[
17
]
, we prove that
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
,
n\geq 2
, is a division algebra and a central simple algebra of PI degree
l^{N(n-1)-m}
.

Proof.

The center of
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
contains
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
, so the finite-dimensionality of
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
over
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
implies the finite-dimensionality of
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
over its center. Since it has no non-zero divisors, this proves
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
is a division algebra.

Now denote by
\Delta^{(n)}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
,
n\geq 2
, the
n
-fold coproduct, i.e.,
\Delta^{(2)}:=\Delta
, the standard coproduct of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
, and
\Delta^{(n)}:=({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta^{(n-1)}
for
n\geq 3
. Identifying
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
as a vector space, we consider
\Delta^{(n)}
as a map
\Delta^{(n)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. It is an algebra morphism
[
18
, Proposition 6.18]
, injective because
\big(\varepsilon^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\Delta^{(n)}={\rm id}
. Then it extends uniquely to the fraction algebra
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. As noted above,
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\!\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
. Since
{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})}
is a Hopf subalgebra of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}

[
41
, Proposition 6.4]
,
\Delta^{(n)}
maps
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{\otimes n}
. Then, extending the scalars of
\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
by the field
Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, consider the algebra

\displaystyle Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big):={}

\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\Delta^{(n)}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))}\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\Delta^{(n)}(Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})))}\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\Delta^{(n)}(Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})))}\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{\Delta^{(n)}(Q(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})))}\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr).

By Proposition
5.2
,
\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
is a
\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr)
-central simple algebra. The left factor is a field, so
Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
is a central simple algebra over it (see, e.g.,
[
96
, Theorem 1.7.27]
, or
[
101
, Lemma 4.9]
). Note that the left factor can also be written as

\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\Delta^{(n)}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))}\Delta^{(n)}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)

for it contains
\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
, it is contained in its fraction field, and
\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
is a field because
\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
is finite over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
and has no nontrivial zero divisors. Note that

\big[\tilde{Q}(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]=l^{m}.

We proved in
[
18
, Proposition 6.19]
that the ring
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n)}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
; the same arguments show that
\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. So the algebra

Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big):=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigotimes_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}

is the centralizer of
Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
in
Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)
. Since the latter is simple, we can apply the double centralizer theorem (see, e.g.,
[
96
, Theorem 7.1.9]
, or
[
101
, Theorem 7.1]
):
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
is a simple algebra, we have

\displaystyle\big[Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\bigl[Q\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]}{\bigl[Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]}=l^{2nN-(2N+m)},

and the centralizer of
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
is
Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
. In particular,
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
has center
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)\cap Q_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\Delta^{(n)}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\big)
, which is easily shown to be
\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)
. It then follows

\displaystyle\big[Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big):\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\big[Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]}{\big[\tilde{Q}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr)\big]}

\displaystyle=l^{2nN-(2N+m)}.l^{-m}=l^{2(N(n-1)-m)}.

Therefore,
Q\big(\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\bigr)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)
is a central simple algebra of PI degree
l^{N(n-1)-m}
.
∎

Appendix A
Low and up crystal structures in the
\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}}
case

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and denote by
V_{k}
the simple
U_{q}^{\rm ad}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
module of dimension
k+1
. It has a basis
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
such that

\displaystyle K.v_{j}=q^{k-2j}v_{j},\qquad F.v_{j}=[j+1]_{q}v_{j+1}\quad\mathrm{if}\quad j<k,\qquad F.v_{k}=0,

\displaystyle E.v_{j}=[k-j+1]_{q}v_{j-1}\quad\mathrm{if}\quad j>0,\qquad E.v_{0}=0.

This basis defines the full
A
-sublattice

{}_{A}V_{k}
, which is left invariant by
U_{A}^{\rm res}
, and we have

F^{(a)}.v_{j}=\left[\begin{matrix}j+a\\
a\end{matrix}\right]_{q}v_{j+a},\qquad E^{(a)}.v_{j}=\left[\begin{matrix}k-j+a\\
a\end{matrix}\right]_{q}v_{j-a},\qquad a\geq 0.

The action of the Kashiwara operator
\tilde{e}
,
\tilde{f}
on
V_{k}
are given by
\tilde{f}(v_{j})=v_{j+1}
,
\tilde{e}(v_{j})=v_{j-1}
.

The crystal basis
\big({\mathcal{L}}^{\rm low},{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm low}\big)
at
q=0
is formed by the
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-sublattice
{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm low}
generated by
{v_{0},\dots,v_{k}}
, and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm low}
by the images
\bar{v}_{0},\dots,\bar{v}_{k}
of these vectors in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm low}/q{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm low}
.

The bilinear form
\langle\,\ \rangle_{k}
defined by (
2.39
) is easily computed

\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle_{k}=\big\langle F^{(i)}.v_{0},F^{(j)}.v_{0}\big\rangle_{k}=\big\langle v_{0},E^{(i)}F^{(j)}.v_{0}\big\rangle_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
i\end{matrix}\right]_{q}\delta_{i,j}.

By definition,

{}_{A}V_{k}^{\rm up}=\{v\in V_{k},\,\langle v,{}_{A}V_{k}\rangle_{k}\subset A\}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{k}Av_{j}^{\rm up},

where

v_{j}^{\rm up}=\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
j\end{matrix}\right]_{q}^{-1}v_{j}.

The upper crystal basis
({\mathcal{L}}^{\rm up},{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm up})
at
q=0
is formed by the
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-sublattice
{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm up}
generated by
{v_{0}^{\rm up},\dots,v_{k}^{\rm up}}
, and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm up}
by the images
\bar{v}_{0}^{\rm up},\dots,\bar{v}_{k}^{\rm up}
of these vectors in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm up}/q{\mathcal{L}}^{\rm up}
.

Using that
[n]_{q}\in q^{1-n}(1+q{\mathcal{A}}_{0})
, we obtain

\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
j\end{matrix}\right]_{q}\in q^{j^{2}-kj}(1+q{\mathcal{A}}_{0}).

As a result, we get
\bar{v}_{j}^{\rm up}=q^{kj-j^{2}}\bar{v}_{j}
, which is exactly the relation (
2.41
) relating the low and up crystal bases, with
\lambda=k\varpi_{1}
,
\mu=(k-2j)\varpi_{1}
.

Appendix B
Quantum Weyl group

We recall some of the formulas of
[
31
]
. Let
e_{q}(z)
be the formal power series in
z
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}(q)
defined by

e_{q}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{(n)_{q^{2}}!}.

We first consider the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
. As explained in
[
18
, Section 3]
, the Cartan element
{H\in\mathfrak{g}}
defines an element of
{\mathbb{U}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
. Viewed as elements of
{\mathbb{U}}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
we have
L\!=\!q^{H/2}
. The series
{\Theta=q^{H\otimes H/2}}
defines an element of
{\mathbb{U}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})^{\hat{\otimes}2}
, its image under multiplication being
q^{H^{2}/2}
. The
R
-matrix can be expressed as

R=\Theta\hat{R}
where
\hat{R}=e_{q^{-1}}\big(\big(q-q^{-1}\big)E\otimes F\big)
is a well defined element of
{\mathbb{U}}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\hat{\otimes}2}
.
Consider the Lusztig
[
82
]
braid group automorphism of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
, defined by

T(L)=L^{-1},\qquad T(E)=-FK^{-1},\qquad T(F)=-KE.

(B.1)

For every
x\in U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
it satisfies:
\Delta(T(x))=\hat{R}^{-1}(T\otimes T)(\Delta(x))\hat{R}
.
Define the quantum Weyl group element
\hat{w}\in{\mathbb{U}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
by Saito’s formula
[
97
]
:

\hat{w}=e_{q^{-1}}(F)q^{-H^{2}/4}e_{q^{-1}}(-E)q^{-H^{2}/4}e_{q^{-1}}(F)q^{-H/2}.

(B.2)

For every
x\in U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
, it satisfies

\displaystyle T(x)=\hat{w}x\hat{w}^{-1},\qquad\Delta(\hat{w})=\hat{R}^{-1}(\hat{w}\otimes\hat{w}),

(B.3)

\displaystyle\hat{w}^{2}=q^{H^{2}/2}\xi\theta,

(B.4)

where
\theta\in{\mathbb{U}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
is the ribbon element, and
\xi\in{\mathbb{U}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
is the central group element whose value on the type
1
simple module
V_{k}
of
U_{q}^{\rm ad}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
of dimension
k+1
is the scalar endomorphism
{(-1)^{k}id_{V_{k}}}
.

In order to compare our setting to the one of
[
41
]
, we need an explicit formula of
\hat{w}
.
Using the basis
v_{j}
of
V_{k}
of Appendix
A
, (
B.1
), (
B.3
) and (
B.4
), we obtain

\hat{w}{v}_{j}=(-1)^{j}q^{-j(k-j-1)-k}{v}_{k-j}.

(B.5)

In
[
41
]
, another quantum Weyl group element
\underline{w}
is defined. It is dual to the Vaksman–Soibelman functional
t\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q)
of
[
98
,
102
]
, that is,
t(\alpha)=\langle\alpha,\underline{w}\rangle
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})
. By comparing (
B.5
) with the formulas defining the action of
t
in
[
41
, Section 1.7]
, we find
\underline{w}=\xi\hat{w}K
and the basis vectors
w_{r}^{p}
of
[
41
]
, where
p\in(1/2)\mathbb{N}
and
r\in\{-p,-p+1,\dots,p-1,p\}
, are related to the vectors
v_{j}
above as follows:

{v}_{j}=\lambda_{j}w_{r}^{p}
,
where
k=2p
,
j=p-r
,
\lambda_{0}=1
,
\lambda_{1}=[k]q^{-k}
, and

\lambda_{j}=\frac{[k]!}{[j]![k-(j-2)]!}q^{j(j+1)-j(k+2)},\qquad j\geq 2.

Explicit formulas of the evaluation of
t
on basis vectors of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})
can be computed. We get

\displaystyle t\big(\tilde{a}^{\star m}\star\tilde{b}^{\star n}\star\tilde{d}^{\star p}\big)=\delta_{m,p}q^{-np}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\big(1-q^{-2i}\big),

(B.6)

\displaystyle t\big(\tilde{a}^{\star m}\star\tilde{c}^{\star n}\star\tilde{d}^{\star p}\big)=(-1)^{n}\delta_{m,p}q^{-n(p+1)}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\big(1-q^{-2i}\big),

(B.7)

where
\tilde{a}=a
,
\tilde{b}=qb
,
\tilde{c}=q^{-1}c
,
\tilde{d}=d
and as usual
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
are the standard generators of
\mathcal{O}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})
, i.e., the matrix coefficients in the basis of weight vectors
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
of the
2
-dimensional irreducible representation
V_{1}
of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
such that
K.v_{0}=qv_{0}
and
v_{1}=F.v_{0}
. Here we have introduced the generators
\tilde{a},\dots,\tilde{d}
to facilitate the comparison with the formulas in
[
41
]
; these generators come naturally in their setup because they use different generators
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, which in our notations can be written respectively as
K_{i}^{-1}E_{i}
and
F_{i}K_{i}
.

The formulas (
B.6
)–(
B.7
) can be shown by two independent methods. The first uses a definition of
t
as a
GNS
state associated to an infinite-dimensional representation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\rm SL}_{2})
, as recalled in
[
41
, Section 1.6]
. The second is to write, e.g.,

t\big(\tilde{a}^{\star m}\star\tilde{b}^{\star n}\star\tilde{d}^{\star p}\big)=\big\langle\tilde{a}^{\otimes m}\otimes\tilde{b}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tilde{d}^{\otimes p},\Delta^{(m+n+p)}(\underline{w})\big\rangle

(B.8)

and to use explicit expressions of
\Delta^{(m+n+p)}(\underline{w})
when represented on
V_{1}^{\otimes(m+n+p)}
. In general, one can check that

\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}(\hat{\omega})={}

\displaystyle\big(\Delta^{(n-1)}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)\big(\big(\Delta^{(n-2)}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)\otimes{\rm id}\big)\cdots\big(\big(\Delta\otimes{\rm id}\big)\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)\otimes{\rm id}^{\otimes(n-3)}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\otimes{\rm id}^{\otimes(n-2)}\big)\hat{\omega}^{\otimes n}.

By (
B.5
) or (
B.6
)–(
B.7
), we see that
\hat{w}
(or
\underline{w}
) and
t
are well defined on the integral forms,

\hat{w}\in\mathbb{U}_{\Gamma},\qquad t\colon\ {\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\rm SL}_{2})\rightarrow A.

We now consider the case where
\mathfrak{g}
is of rank
m\geq 2
. To each simple root
\alpha_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, is associated the subalgebra of
U_{q}
generated by
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
L_{i}
,
L_{i}^{-1}
. It is a copy of
U_{q_{i}}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
, where
{q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}}
. Let
\hat{w}_{i}
be the corresponding quantum Weyl group element in
\mathbb{U}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, defined by Saito’s formula (
B.2
), replacing
H
,
E
,
F
by
H_{i}
,
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
. Also, denote by
\nu_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{q_{i}}({\rm SL}_{2})
the projection map dual to the inclusion
U_{q_{i}}({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})\bigotimes_{\mathbb{C}(q_{i})}\mathbb{C}(q)\hookrightarrow U_{q}
, and put
t_{i}=t\circ\nu_{i}
. Let
\underline{w}_{i}
be the corresponding quantum Weyl group element in
\mathbb{U}_{q}
, i.e.,
t_{i}(\alpha)=\langle\alpha,\underline{w}_{i}\rangle
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
. On integral forms they yield well-defined elements
\hat{w}_{i},\underline{w}_{i}\in\mathbb{U}_{\Gamma}
and
t_{i}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\rightarrow A
(see
[
41
, Proposition 5.1]
, and
[
84
]
for a different construction). They satisfy the defining relations of the braid group
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{g}

[
70
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{i}\hat{w}_{j}\hat{w}_{i}=\hat{w}_{j}\hat{w}_{i}\hat{w}_{j}\qquad\text{if}\quad a_{ij}a_{ji}=1,

\displaystyle(\hat{w}_{i}\hat{w}_{j})^{k}=(\hat{w}_{j}\hat{w}_{i})^{k}\qquad\text{for}\quad k=1,2,3\quad\text{if}\quad a_{ij}a_{ji}=0,2,3,

and similarly by replacing
\hat{w}_{i}
with
\underline{w}_{i}
, or with
t_{i}
(see
[
98
]
for the latter). The Weyl group
W=W(\mathfrak{g})=N(T_{G})/T_{G}
is generated by the reflections
s_{i}
associated to the simple roots
\alpha_{i}
. Denote by
n_{i}\in N(T_{G})
a representative of
s_{i}
. Let
w\in W
and denote by
w=s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{k}}
a reduced expression. Because of the braid group relations the elements
\hat{w}=\hat{w}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{w}_{i_{k}}
,
\underline{w}=\underline{w}_{i_{1}}\cdots\underline{w}_{i_{k}}
and the functional
t_{w}=t_{i_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k}}

do not depend on the choice of reduced expression. The Lusztig
[
