82
]
braid group automorphism
T_{w}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma
associated to
w
satisfies (see
[
41
]
)

T_{w}(x)=\hat{w}x\hat{w}^{-1},\qquad x\in\Gamma.

Let
w_{0}
be the longest element in
W
. We have

\Delta(\hat{w}_{0})=\hat{R}^{-1}(\hat{w}_{0}\otimes\hat{w}_{0}),

(B.9)

where as usual
R={\Theta}{\hat{R}}
.

Appendix C
Regular action on
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}}

The following result is proved in
[
41
, Section 1.10]
. For completeness, let us give a (different) proof. Recall from (
2.72
) that we may identify
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
with
{\mathcal{O}}(G)
.

Proposition C.1
.

For every
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
,
g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle t_{i}(f)=f(n_{i}),

(C.1)

\displaystyle t_{i}(f\star g)=t_{i}(f)t_{i}(g).

(C.2)

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the results for
{\rm SL}_{2}
because
\nu_{i}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon_{i}}({\rm SL}_{2})
is a morphism of Hopf algebras and
\nu_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}))\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon_{i}}({\rm SL}_{2}))
. In this case, (
C.1
) can be proved by using
(
B.6
)–(
B.7
)
, evaluating
t
on basis elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
as is done in
[
41
, Lemma 1.5 (a)]
. Such a basis is formed by monomials like in (
B.6
)–(
B.7
), with all exponents divisible by
l
; then for instance

t\big(\tilde{a}^{\star ml}\star\tilde{b}^{\star nl}\star\tilde{d}^{\star pl}\big)=\delta_{p,0}\delta_{m,0}=\underline{a}^{m}\underline{b}^{n}\underline{d}^{p}(n),

where
\underline{a},\dots,\underline{d}
are the generators of
{\mathcal{O}}(G)={\mathcal{O}}_{1}(G)
corresponding to
a,\dots,d
, and we take

n=\left(\begin{matrix}\hphantom{-}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)

as representative of the reflection
s
generating the Weyl group
W({\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
. Here is an alternative proof of (
C.1
): (
C.2
) shows that
t
is a homomorphism on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
, so by proving (
C.2
) at first one is reduced to check (
C.1
) on the generators
a^{\star l},\dots,d^{\star l}
, which is easy by means of (
B.8
) and (
B.9
).

We provide a proof of (
C.2
) that we find more conceptual than the one in
[
41
, Lemma 1.5 (b)]
(which uses again (
B.6
)–(
B.7
)). As above, let us denote
\underline{w}=\xi\hat{w}K
. For any
f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle t(f\star g)

\displaystyle=(f\otimes g)(\Delta(\underline{w}))=(f\otimes g)\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}(\underline{w}\otimes\underline{w})\big)=\sum_{(\widehat{R}^{-1})}f\big(\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)_{(1)}\underline{w}\big)g\big(\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)_{(2)}\underline{w}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\widehat{R}^{-1}),(f)}f_{(1)}\big(\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)_{(1)}\big)f_{(2)}(\underline{w})g\big(\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)_{(2)}\underline{w}\big)=\sum_{(f)}f_{(2)}(\underline{w})g\big((f_{(1)}\otimes{\rm id})\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)\underline{w}\big).

Assume now
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))
is a Hopf subalgebra of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2})
, we have
{f_{(1)}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}({\rm SL}_{2}))}
. From Theorem
2.29
(2), we deduce

(f_{(1)}\otimes{\rm id})\big(\widehat{R}^{-1}\big)\in U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm ad}\big).

Denote by
z
this element. Note that from its expression we have
\epsilon(z)=\epsilon(f_{(1)})
. Now

g(z\underline{w})=\sum_{(g)}g_{(1)}(z)g_{(2)}(\underline{w})
, but
g_{(1)}
is a linear combination of matrix elements of
\Gamma
-modules, on which
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\big(U_{\epsilon}^{\rm ad}\big)
acts by the trivial character. Therefore,

g(z\underline{w})=\sum_{(g)}\epsilon(z)g_{(1)}(1)g_{(2)}(\underline{w})=\epsilon(z)g(\underline{w})=\epsilon(f_{(1)})g(\underline{w}),

and eventually

t(f\star g)=\sum_{(f)}f_{(2)}(\underline{w})\epsilon(f_{(1)})g(\underline{w})=t(f)t(g).

This concludes the proof.
∎

For the sake of completeness, let us show how this result implies:

Proof of Proposition
2.30
(i.e.,
[
41
, Proposition 7.1]
).

We have
f\lhd t_{i}=\sum_{(f)}t_{i}(f_{(1)})f_{(2)}
,
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
is a Hopf subalgebra of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
,
f_{(2)}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
and therefore the maps
\lhd t_{i}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
preserve
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
. Moreover,
(f\lhd t_{i})(a)=\sum_{(f)}f_{(1)}(n_{i})f_{(2)}(a)=f(n_{i}a)
,
a\in G
, by (
C.1
).

It remains to show that
(f\star\alpha)\lhd t_{i}=(f\lhd t_{i})(\alpha\lhd t_{i})
for every
f\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
. We have

\displaystyle(f\star g)\lhd t_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f\star g)}t_{i}\left((f\star g)_{(1)}\right)(f\star g)_{(2)}=\sum_{(f),(g)}t_{i}\left(f_{(1)}\star g_{(1)}\right)f_{(2)}\star g_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f),(g)}t\left(\nu_{i}(f_{(1)})\nu_{i}(g_{(1)})\right)f_{(2)}\star g_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f),(g)}t\left(\nu_{i}(f_{(1)})\right)t\left(\nu_{i}(g_{(1)})\right)f_{(2)}\star g_{(2)},

(C.3)

using that
\nu_{i}
is a homomorphism in the third equality, and (
C.2
) in the last one. The result is just
(f\lhd t_{i})(g\lhd t_{i})
.
∎
